| 2026-08-24 | Phase 2, Task 4 (MetricFlow semantic layer) — `data-platform/dbt/canopica_warehouse/models/semantic/{semantic_models,metrics,semantic,metricflow_time_spine.sql}`, `mart_worker_caseload.sql` (+`as_of_date`), `gold.yml`, `dbt_project.yml`, `pyproject.toml` (+`dbt-metricflow`), `infra/postgres/init/01-databases.sql` (+`canopica_analytics_ro`), new `tests/test_metric_semantics.py` | Design doc approved first (`2026-08-24-analytics-semantic-layer-execution-and-authorization.md`, Option A) per this project's own brainstorm-before-code convention — see the roadmap/tradeoffs/decisions-table edits in the prior commit. **Three real corrections found by running the real tool, not by re-reading the plan**, the same shape as Task 3's five: (1) MetricFlow refused to parse at all without a day-granularity time spine somewhere in the project (`dbt parse`'s own error named this; not in the Task 4 plan) — added `metricflow_time_spine.sql` via dbt-core's built-in `dbt.date_spine()` macro, bounded on a rolling window around `current_date` rather than a hardcoded range so it never goes stale. (2) `mart_determination_outcomes` is pre-aggregated (grouped by five columns) with no natural single-row key, and MetricFlow's own validator refused it with no primary entity — fixed with a `primary_entity:` pointer that doesn't map to a physical column, exactly the pattern MetricFlow's docs describe for this case. (3) `mart_worker_caseload` is a live snapshot (rebuilt wholesale every pipeline run, no history) with no time column at all, and MetricFlow's validator refused its `active_case_count` measure with "aggregation time dimension... is not set" — real evidence that every measure needs one, contradicting an initial assumption it could be skipped for a non-temporal mart; fixed by adding a genuine `as_of_date` column (`current_date`) to the mart itself, the minimal honest way to model "this snapshot's own timestamp" for a table that has never had one. **A fourth correction, in the test file's own location**: the plan named `ai/tests/test_metric_semantics.py`, but the fixture pattern it explicitly calls for (Testcontainers Postgres, the dbt CLI, `DBT_PROJECT_DIR`) only exists in `data-platform`'s own `conftest.py` and dependency set — `ai/` has neither. Filed at `data-platform/tests/test_metric_semantics.py` instead. **`canopica_analytics_ro` verified for real against a throwaway `docker run` of the actual init script**, not assumed from the SQL alone (`docker exec` as each role in turn): SELECT on `reporting.*` succeeds, INSERT is refused (`permission denied for table probe`) — and a real gap the first pass missed, caught only by actually trying it: Postgres grants `CONNECT` to `PUBLIC` on every database by default, so `canopica_analytics_ro` could connect to the PII-bearing `canopica_operational` database with zero table grants needed to notice. Fixed with an explicit `revoke connect on database canopica_operational from public`, then re-verified: `canopica_app` (the database owner) still connects fine, `canopica_analytics_ro` now gets `FATAL: permission denied for database "canopica_operational"`. The real local proof the design doc's own Step 4 calls for: `mf query --metrics avg_processing_days --group-by determination__is_expedited` against a real dbt-built warehouse (Testcontainers-seeded via the existing `seeded_timeliness_dsn` fixture) returns the exact correct averages (expedited 6.5 days, standard 25.0 days) matching that fixture's own known 3/10/15/35-day scenarios — proving the semantic layer is wired to real data, not just schema-valid. Group-by/order-by needed entity-qualified dimension names (`determination__is_expedited`, not bare `is_expedited`) — found from the query resolver's own error and its suggested-names list, not guessed. The plan's own illustrative query example (`--group-by program_code`) doesn't apply to this mart at all — `mart_processing_timeliness` has no `program_code` column — substituted `is_expedited`, the dimension that actually exists and is the meaningful one for a timeliness metric. **Verified:** `make test` green across the monorepo (Java 107, web 20, data-platform **29** — up from 27, the two new tests above — `ai/` 44, unchanged), `make lint` clean. `docs/STATUS.md` updated in this same commit; `README.md`'s architecture diagram checked — no change, it already names "Semantic Layer (MetricFlow · TMDL)" inside the existing Reporting subgraph, same as Tasks 1-3's own calls for capabilities implemented behind an already-named box. **Confirmed in CI** (run `32769404659`, this exact commit): all 11 jobs green -- the confirmation this row's own text called for was left unrecorded when the session moved directly into Task 5 before the scheduled check landed; caught and closed out here rather than left silently missing. |
| 2026-08-24 | **Root cause found** for the recurring "Memory Circuit Breaker is open" failures, plus CI wall-clock work -- `ai/.../corpus/search_pipeline.py`, new `ai/tests/test_search_pipeline.py`, `ai/tests/test_policy_qa.py`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml` | **ml-commons has two memory circuit breakers and both emit the byte-for-byte identical message**, which is why this took five attempts across two days to attribute. `plugins.ml_commons.native_memory_threshold` measures *host* RAM; `plugins.ml_commons.jvm_heap_memory_threshold` measures *JVM heap*. This project's own `ensure_ml_commons_settings()` has raised the first to 99 since Task 1 and never touched the second, which sat at its default of **85**. Read back off the live cluster rather than inferred: `persistent.plugins.ml_commons.native_memory_threshold: "99"` alongside `defaults.plugins.ml_commons.jvm_heap_memory_threshold: "85"`. With the CI runner at 92% host memory against a 99% threshold, the host breaker was never the one tripping -- and the failing responses say so, reporting `bytes_wanted: 0, bytes_limit: 0`, which is ml-commons' own breaker rather than the JVM's byte-accounted indices breakers. Measured on this stack's own container: with the cross-encoder reranker deployed into the 2g heap, heap sits at **73% immediately after indexing, before any query load**, one allocation burst from 85. **This means every fix attempted so far targeted the wrong resource** -- freeing host services, the `--force-recreate` workaround, splitting the e2e jobs, quantizing Ollama's KV cache to q8_0 (the row above, which measured a real 209MB saving and did not fix CI, exactly as that row was careful not to promise) -- all of them free *host* memory, which was never the constraint. It also disposes of a migration this session was about to recommend: moving generation off Ollama to a hosted API would have freed ~2.3GiB of host RAM, added a vendor dependency and an API key, and **left the build red**, because the breaker measures heap percentage. Recorded prominently because the failure was legible the whole time and the wrong variable was optimized five times. Fixed by setting `plugins.ml_commons.jvm_heap_memory_threshold: "92"` in the same call that already sets the host one -- verified applied against the live cluster (`acknowledged: true`, then read back as `"92"`), and deliberately below OpenSearch's own parent breaker (`indices.breaker.total.limit`, confirmed at 95%), which stays as the backstop against actually exhausting the JVM. **The user's suggestion to shrink OpenSearch's footprint, measured in both halves rather than accepted or dismissed.** Reducing the heap would have made this *worse*, not better: at `-Xms1g -Xmx1g` the reranker puts heap at **83%** against an 85% breaker, and at 512m it cannot load at all -- which is the measurement behind the docker-compose comment that previously only asserted it. Trimming plugins is real and is the half that works: a purpose-built image with 16 unused plugins removed (alerting, anomaly-detection, security, sql, observability and the rest) measured **2.651GiB -> 2.281GiB, 370MB saved** at identical heap. Deliberately **not** applied here, with the reason stated rather than left implicit: it frees host RAM, which this row has just established is not the binding resource, and it costs a Docker build in both e2e jobs against the same request's explicit speed goal. It is the next lever if 92 proves insufficient, not a discarded idea. **A second, latent bug fixed with it**, and a claim of mine corrected in the process: `ensure_reranker_model()` looked up the model with `model_state: DEPLOYED` in the query, so a model in any other state was invisible and got registered again rather than reused. `DEPLOY_FAILED` is precisely what a tripped breaker leaves behind, so the run *after* a breaker trip would register a second ~90MB copy into a cluster that had just run out of heap. I had reported this as "10 duplicate registered copies found live"; checking rather than repeating it, those ten documents are the **content chunks of one model** (`<id>_0` … `<id>_9`, all sharing the parent id, no `model_state`, no `model_group_id`) -- ml-commons chunks a model's ONNX bytes across its own index. There was one model, not ten. The bug is real and reachable, the evidence I gave for it was not. Fixed by splitting registration and deployment into two decisions; verified against the live cluster by running `setup()` twice and confirming the model count stayed at 11 and the same `model_id` came back both times. **CI cannot catch this class of bug at all** -- its OpenSearch volume is created fresh every run, so there is never a second run to be wrong about -- which is why the new `ai/tests/test_search_pipeline.py` drives it through a fake at the transport seam. TDD throughout, including one round on the test itself: the first fake ignored the query body, so it would have answered a `DEPLOYED` lookup with an undeployed model and passed against the very code it exists to reject; it now applies the query's own `term` clauses, and the test then failed for the right reason (`assert 'freshly-registered-model' == 'registered-model'`) before the fix. **Diagnostics, so the next occurrence names itself instead of costing another round of inference:** an `if: failure()` step dumps the thresholds actually in force, heap used, and every breaker's tripped count, plus OpenSearch's own log tail -- both `jq` expressions were run against the live cluster and corrected (the first crashed on `$p[-1]` being an array index) rather than committed unverified. `df -h /` added to the always-on step so the third breaker, free disk, has a baseline on green runs too. **`timeout-minutes` on all 11 jobs**, which the repo had nowhere -- from the article the user supplied, whose point that "Ollama can hang silently" is exactly the failure a 6-hour default turns into a wasted day. **Wall clock, measured from the last green run's own step timings** (`32686949429`, e2e-ai 1044s of which 353s was setup before the first test ran): freeing ~17GB of unused preinstalled toolchains (83s, disk-bound) ran back to back with pulling ~10GB of images (91s, network-bound) for no reason -- they are independent and now run concurrently, `max(83, 91)` instead of `174`. And `test_policy_qa.py` paid for two redundant real generations: `TestAnswerGeneral` ran the same question twice to ask two different questions about the answer, and `TestAnswerDenial` submitted two applications, ran two DMN determinations and generated two explanations to assert two properties. Both hoisted into class-scoped fixtures -- no test merged, no assertion weakened, and the persistence test genuinely strengthened, since "exactly one stored explanation for this determination" now pins one-call-one-row instead of only ever seeing a determination created moments earlier for its exclusive use. A single RAG generation on the 4-vCPU runner is dominated by prompt evaluation over the retrieved context, not token output, and the suite's own step timings put `test_policy_qa.py` at 529s of the 683s total. **One self-inflicted bug caught by running the suite rather than reasoning about it.** The first version of the shared fixture reused the provenance test's uuid-suffixed question, and both `TestAnswerGeneral` tests then failed. The uuid was never harmless -- `answer_general` passes the question verbatim to BM25 retrieval, to the k-NN embedding, *and* into the prompt, so a random token degrades all three -- it had simply only ever been paid for by the one test that needed a unique row. Sharing it handed that cost to the grounding test as well. Fixed by keeping the question as clean prose and having the provenance test find its row by recency (`order by created_at desc limit 1`) instead, which removes the uuid from the system entirely rather than moving it. Both tests pass after. **Verified on the committed state:** `make test` green across the monorepo -- Java 12 (rules engine) + 107 (portal), web 20/20, data-platform 27, `ai/` **44** (up from 40, the four new `test_search_pipeline.py` cases) -- and `make lint` clean. `ai/`'s own e2e **15 of 16 in 195.61s**, the one failure being `TestProposeAgainstARealModel` with an Ollama 500, which is the same dev-host-only both-models-resident OOM already recorded two rows up and green in CI on its own commit; this host runs Ollama at 3.475GiB beside OpenSearch at 2.457GiB on a 7.654GiB VM, which is a host-memory problem and specifically *not* the heap breaker this row fixes. **Stated without overclaiming, for the third time in this log and the second on this exact failure: one green run will not prove the breaker fix.** The mechanism is now identified rather than guessed at, which is a different quality of evidence than the previous two attempts had, but the standing rule holds -- this needs several consecutive green runs before anyone calls it settled, and the diagnostics above exist precisely so the next failure is attributable in one run instead of five. |
| 2026-08-24 | **Correction + fix**: the `num_ctx` raise tipped OpenSearch's memory circuit breaker in CI -- `infra/docker-compose.yml` (`OLLAMA_KV_CACHE_TYPE=q8_0`) | **I called the previous row's CI result durable on one green run, and the very next run disproved it.** Run `32686949429` (`ae1e1df`) was green on all 11 jobs, and I recorded that the `num_ctx` increase's extra ~235MB "holds". The mechanism is real rather than coincidental: ml-commons' native memory circuit breaker thresholds on a percentage of **host** memory, not heap, so Ollama growing is directly what trips OpenSearch. The raise itself is not negotiable -- at 2048 the runtime was silently truncating real prompts -- so the fix pays the memory back instead of reverting correctness. Measured, not assumed: llama3.2:3b at num_ctx=4096 uses **2.733GiB with the f16 KV cache default and 2.524GiB at q8_0**, returning ~209MB of the ~235MB the raise cost, and Ollama's own launch line was read back to confirm `--cache-type-k q8_0 --cache-type-v q8_0` rather than trusting that the variable was honored. q8_0 (not the more aggressive q4_0) with flash attention on. **Stated without overclaiming this time:** this restores the margin that existed before the raise; it does not eliminate the class of failure, and the circuit breaker predates this change (it is why OpenSearch's heap was pinned to 2g earlier). It needs more than one green run before anyone calls it settled. |
| 2026-08-23 | Money crossing the wire as a JSON number -- `JacksonConfig` (new), `WorkerCaseControllerTest`, `types.ts`, `ProposedParameterValue`/`CurrentParameterValue` | Noticed while reviewing Task 3's own DTOs and fixed on its own commit rather than folded in. `portal/web/src/api/types.ts` has declared `benefitAmount: string` since Phase 1a with the comment "money never round-trips through a float" -- and it was **untrue at exactly the last hop**: Jackson serialises `BigDecimal` as a bare JSON number by default, so `JSON.parse` turned a $649.00 award into the double `649` before any component saw it. Every other layer already held the invariant (numeric(12,4) in Postgres, BigDecimal through the rules engine and DTOs, decimal strings from the synthetic loader on the way in) -- the one place it broke was serialisation out, which is also the only place nothing asserted it. Proven before fixing: the new test failed with `benefitAmount as JSON string / Expecting value to be true but was false`, i.e. a number on the wire. Fixed with a single `Jackson2ObjectMapperBuilderCustomizer` registering a `BigDecimal` serialiser that writes `toPlainString()`, rather than `@JsonFormat(shape = STRING)` per field: every BigDecimal in this system is a dollar amount or a rate, both want to be strings, and a convention enforced by remembering an annotation lasts until the next DTO -- the two annotations Task 3 had just added were deleted as redundant. `toPlainString()` specifically, so a figure never renders in scientific notation and the stored scale survives ("649.00", not "649"). Deserialisation deliberately untouched: Jackson already reads a JSON string into a BigDecimal losslessly, which is why intake has accepted string amounts since Phase 1a. Visible user-facing effect: benefit amounts render with cents again. `make test` green (Java 12 + **107**, web 20, data-platform 27, `ai/` 40), `make lint` clean. |

That is the hypothesis above tested rather than assumed, and it holds even with the `num_ctx` increase's extra ~235MB. **Two real infrastructure findings came out of chasing it, both verified rather than assumed.** (1) `num_ctx` was never set, so it defaulted to 2048, and Ollama silently truncates past it: the same 12,186-character prompt reported `prompt_eval_count` of **1,026 at num_ctx=2048 versus 3,105 at 4,096**, answering `200 OK` both times. The rule-authoring prompt puts the parameter list at the *front*, so the copilot would have been diffing against a list it never saw. Fixed three ways: explicit settings-driven `num_ctx`, a pre-send guard that refuses a prompt that would not fit (checked *before* the request, because `prompt_eval_count` cannot detect truncation afterwards -- prompt caching lowers it identically), and an excerpt cap cut from a guessed 8,000 characters to a measured 6,000. (2) Ollama 0.32's llama.cpp prompt cache defaults to an **8GB** ceiling on a 7.65GB VM, and was observed at `cache state: 3 prompts, 584.235 MiB` and climbing. Capped to 512MB via `LLAMA_ARG_CACHE_RAM`, which Ollama exposes no `OLLAMA_*` variable for -- verified honored by watching the limit in that same log line change from `8192.000 MiB` to `512.000 MiB`, not inferred from documentation. It bounded the cache as intended (381MB of 512 during the run) and did **not** fix the failure above, which is recorded because a fix that did not work is worth as much as one that did. **Design-decision detour:** publishing a superseding parameter set was impossible as the schema stood (the seeded FY2026 set is open-ended and V3's trigger refused every UPDATE), which is a decision rather than an implementation detail, so it went through the design-doc process at the user's explicit direction -- `docs/design/2026-08-23-policy-parameter-supersession.md`, recorded in the roadmap §3.3 and the decisions table above. Implementing it surfaced that V3's single shared trigger function cannot be narrowed per-table at all: PL/pgSQL plans a whole boolean expression up front, so a `tg_table_name` guard does not stop `record "old" has no field "effective_to"` firing for `policy_parameter`. Split into one function per table. |

Root cause is the instruction itself: "cite the exact section number(s) you used" names no particular section, so a terse answer satisfies it by citing nothing. Measured cost of that thrashing: **330s vs 190s** on the same suite, a 74% regression on precisely the step the previous row set out to speed up. And it buys nothing where it would have to pay off -- checked both lean-stack CI runs for `signal: killed` and found **zero**: those OOM kills are specific to this dev Docker VM (~7.65GB, shared with everything else running), not the runner (~7.8GB with its own lean stack). Verified on the exact committed state: `ai/` e2e 15/15 (230.61s), `make lint` clean, `make test` green across the monorepo. `make test` needed a second run to get that green, worth recording because the first attempt's 34 Java errors looked alarming and were not a code defect at all: `ContainerLaunchException ... quay.io/keycloak/keycloak:26.4` timing out, because the e2e Compose stack (OpenSearch 2.5GB + Ollama 3GB) was still resident while Testcontainers tried to start its *own* Postgres and Keycloak on the same ~7.65GB VM. At `temperature=0.2` the same three questions cited real sections every time. The suite then failed 2/15 on abstentions, and Ollama's own logs gave the reason -- `llama-server process no longer running ... signal: killed`, i.e. the inference process was OOM-killed mid-suite. Extending `keep_alive` keeps the embedding model resident alongside the generation model on a host that is already memory-tight, and buys nothing here because this suite's generation calls run back to back and stay warm inside the 5m default anyway. Reverted to 5m, kept as a tunable setting for a deployment with real headroom. | 2026-08-23 | CI wall-clock optimization -- CI only (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`), no app/pipeline code | User-flagged as first priority: every commit was blocking on a ~21min pipeline, and the idle waiting was materially slowing development. Measured before changing anything, rather than optimizing by intuition -- per-job timings across three real runs showed every job except `e2e` finishing under 2min, with `e2e` at 1130-1259s, so `e2e` alone was >90% of wall clock. Step-level breakdown of `e2e` (run `32669212801`): `ai/`'s pytest 601s, Compose bring-up 450s (114s image builds + 198s health-wait + 138s model pull), disk cleanup 77s, `data-platform`'s pytest 62s, plus ~120s of `needs:` gating before the job even started. Three changes, each aimed at a measured cost: (1) **Split the one serial `e2e` job into two parallel jobs on lean stacks.** The combined job stood up all 14 services, ran `data-platform`'s e2e, then *stopped seven of them* and ran `ai/`'s -- each suite waiting on services the other needs and it never touches. Confirmed from the suites' own port usage rather than assumed (`grep` for `localhost:<port>` across each test file): `data-platform`'s e2e hits portal-api/portal-web/metabase/keycloak/airflow-webserver/jaeger and never OpenSearch or Ollama; `ai/`'s hits OpenSearch/Ollama/portal-api (plus postgres/keycloak/jaeger transitively, genuinely needed -- `TestAnswerDenial` submits a real application and decides a real determination through the live portal API under real Keycloak tokens) and never Metabase/Airflow/Grafana/portal-web. Wall clock becomes max(a, b) instead of a + b. Verified the riskier half empirically instead of trusting the port analysis alone: stopped `opensearch` and `ollama` locally and ran `data-platform`'s full e2e -- 9/9 passed, 25.38s. The `ai/` half needs no separate proof, since its lean stack is exactly the post-stop set that two consecutive green CI runs already exercised. A real structural side effect, not just a speedup: dropping OpenSearch's 2g heap and Ollama's ~3GB model out of the data-platform job removes the memory contention behind this task's own three-times-recurring "Memory Circuit Breaker is open" failures at the root, instead of continuing to tune margins around it -- the `--force-recreate` workaround that fix needed is now gone with it. (2) **Skip the two `e2e` jobs when only docs changed.** This repo's own convention commits `docs/STATUS.md` constantly, often as its own commit recording a CI result -- checked against real history, 3 of the last 7 pushes (`dfe2da7`, `ee2696b`, `409ae2f`) were pure `STATUS.md` commits, each spending ~21min proving unchanged code still behaves unchanged. The eight fast jobs still run on a docs-only commit (under 2min total, honoring this project's standard of verifying even a docs-only change); only the expensive pair is gated. Deliberately a deny-list (anything that isn't `docs/**` or a root `*.md` counts as code), not an allow-list of code paths, so a new top-level directory nobody remembered to add still runs e2e -- the failure mode is a wasted run, not a silently skipped one. Validated the classifier against seven real commits plus four edge cases (empty/unknown diff base, root README, mixed docs+code, nested `.md`) before trusting it. The first validation pass reported nonsense (a large code commit classified as docs-only); the cause was the *test harness*, not the logic -- this machine's shell aliases `grep` to `ugrep`, so re-running through `command grep` (what an Ubuntu runner actually uses) gave the correct classification on all eleven cases. Worth recording as a general trap: validating a CI shell snippet on this machine can silently exercise a different `grep` than CI will. (3) **`concurrency` with `cancel-in-progress`.** A quick series of commits was queueing several full e2e runs whose results nobody would read. Trade-off accepted deliberately: a superseded commit on `main` may end without a completed run, which is the right call while iterating but is worth revisiting if this repo ever gains branch protection (see the repo-visibility row -- protection needs GitHub Pro on a private personal repo, so it isn't in play today). Expected wall clock ~12-13min on a code push (bounded by `ai/`'s own 601s test step) and ~2min on a docs-only push, from ~21min for both. Not yet confirmed against a real runner as of this row -- see the next row once pushed. The remaining dominant cost is `ai/`'s 601s pytest step itself, which is ~4 real CPU-bound `llama3.2:3b` generations; the next lever there is capping generation length (`num_predict`, currently unset, so a verbose answer runs unbounded) and/or `OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE` -- deliberately not bundled here, since that changes real AI behavior and needs its own TDD pass against the citation-grounding tests rather than riding along with a CI-config change. |

Switched to a host bind mount (`./ollama-models:/root/.ollama`, i.e. `infra/ollama-models/`) and added an `actions/cache@v6` step with a static key (the compose file changes for unrelated reasons -- memory tuning, service config -- far more often than the pulled model tags do, so a content hash of it would be the wrong cache-invalidation signal). `ollama pull` is already content-addressed and idempotent, so a warm cache turns it into a manifest/digest verification instead of a download -- confirmed locally: migrated the existing named volume's ~2.1GB into the new bind-mount path via a temporary container (`docker run -v infra_ollama-data:/from -v .../infra/ollama-models:/to alpine cp -a`, avoiding a redundant local re-download too), brought `ollama` up against it, and timed `ollama-init`'s own pull step at 2.5s total with both models reported "success" instantly -- versus the multi-minute real downloads this same task's own CI logs already show elsewhere. `infra/ollama-models/` gitignored (several GB, never committed); the now-unused `ollama-data` named volume left in place rather than deleted (orphaned, reclaimable via a manual `docker volume prune` if ever wanted). The IDE flagged `actions/cache@v4` (a first guess) as unresolvable -- checked against the real GitHub API rather than assumed away: `actions/cache` is at v6 (v5 and v6 both postdate v4), matching this workflow's own existing pattern of pinning current majors (`checkout@v7`). One real limit worth stating plainly: the first push after this change still populates the cache from scratch (a full download, same as before) -- the push after that is the one that should show the real speedup. Push (`535a9e7`) reached CI and the job itself went green, but reading the actual log rather than trusting the checkmark (this task's own repeated lesson) found the cache-*save* step had silently failed: `##[warning]Failed to save: ... tar: infra/ollama-models/id_ed25519: Cannot open: Permission denied` (also `cache/model-recommendations.json`) -- Ollama, running as root in its container, writes its own identity key and an internal cache file into the bind mount at 0600, which `actions/cache`'s save step (running as the unprivileged `runner` user) cannot read. A cache-save failure only warns, it doesn't fail the job, so the run showed all-green while quietly never populating the cache at all -- the exact kind of gap only reading real logs catches. First local verification attempt was a false negative, caught before being trusted: Docker Desktop's own bind-mount file sharing on macOS doesn't enforce the same cross-UID POSIX permission checks a native Linux runner does, so testing the 0600 file against an unprivileged container user through the bind mount read it successfully with no fix applied at all. Corrected by reproducing inside Docker's actual Linux VM instead -- copied the same files into a genuine named volume (not a Mac bind mount), confirmed an unprivileged UID gets real `Permission denied` against a root-owned 0600 file there, then confirmed `chmod -R a+rX` on that same volume fixes it. Fix: an `if: always()` step runs `sudo chmod -R a+rX infra/ollama-models` right after `ai/`'s e2e step (before the automatic end-of-job cache-save), so a failed test run still leaves a saveable cache behind. Not yet confirmed against a real GitHub-hosted runner as of this row -- see the next row once pushed. |

Two real corrections found during implementation, not assumed from the plan: (1) **chunking algorithm** -- the plan's own Step 3 describes chunking "at the CFR's own subsection level" via the section numbers alone; a first implementation tried the generic approach (track the last-opened letter, treat a paragraph as a new one only when its marker is the next expected letter) and it silently mis-chunked the real, live-fetched corpus text: 7 CFR 273.2(h) opens its own sub-list directly at a bare roman numeral "(i)" with no numbered level in between, and single-character roman tokens (i/v/x/l/c/d/m) are simultaneously valid next-letters, so that parser folded the real, separate "(i) Expedited service" paragraph (appearing later) into the unrelated (h) sub-item as trailing text -- caught by writing `test_corpus_index.py`'s own collision-specific test against the real corpus, not a contrived fixture, before this was fixed; chunk.py's boundaries are now hand-verified anchors for the top level, with a size-triggered, level-index-strictly-increasing recursive sub-splitter (digit, then roman, then upper-letter, each attempted at most once per branch, each requiring strict "+1 from here" sequencing to avoid the same collision recurring at deeper nesting -- a second, related bug this same design hit and fixed before commit, documented inline in chunk.py's own module docstring) for chunks too large for nomic-embed-text's real ~2048-token context ceiling (hit live, on 273.2(j), before the recursive splitter existed at all). (2) **OpenSearch RRF API** -- the plan's Step 5 describes RRF as configured via "a normalization-processor with technique: rrf"; verified against OpenSearch's current documentation before writing `search_pipeline.json`, this is not how OpenSearch 2.19 (the version this repo pins, chosen specifically for RRF support) exposes it -- RRF is its own `score-ranker-processor` (a `phase_results_processors` stage), not a `normalization-processor` technique. A third, undocumented-by-the-plan fix: ml-commons' local model deployment failed live with "Memory Circuit Breaker is open" against OpenSearch's original 512m-heap Compose config; raised to 1200m (real headroom on an 8GB+ dev machine, confirmed via `docker stats`) resolved it, alongside the real ml-commons cluster settings (`only_run_on_ml_node: false`, `native_memory_threshold: 99`, etc.) `search_pipeline.py`'s own `ensure_ml_commons_settings()` sets idempotently. Corpus is the real, public 7 CFR §273.2 (paragraphs (i) expedited service, (j) categorical eligibility) and §273.9 (paragraph (a) income eligibility standards, paragraph (d)'s five DMN-computed deductions -- standard/earned-income/medical/dependent-care/shelter, cross-checked directly against `rules-engine/src/main/resources/dmn/snap-eligibility.dmn`'s own decision names, not assumed from the design doc's prose), fetched live from eCFR's public Versioner API (`CFR_AS_OF_DATE = 2026-01-01`, pinned for reproducibility) and committed under `ai/src/canopica_ai/policy_intelligence/corpus/raw/`; 46 chunks, no duplicate citation ids, full paragraph coverage (lossless partition), every chunk verified to embed successfully against the real local Ollama server (three chunks landed above the soft `MAX_CHUNK_CHARS` target with no further real CFR marker boundary available to split on -- each individually confirmed, live, to still embed successfully rather than assumed safe from the constant alone). `search_pipeline.py`'s full setup (cluster settings, model group, cross-encoder register+deploy, pipeline creation) verified idempotent by running it twice from a from-scratch teardown (second run: 0.38s, same `model_id`, no re-registration) and reranking verified live to actually change result order versus an RRF-only pipeline (not a no-op), both asserted in `test_retrieval.py` against the real stack. `infra/docker-compose.yml` gains `opensearch` (2.19.6), `ollama` (0.32.15), and a one-shot `ollama-init` pulling the two pinned model tags (`nomic-embed-text`, `llama3.2:3b`) into Ollama's own named volume -- confirmed the `--wait` flag already used for `airflow-init` handles this one-shot service identically (`docker compose up -d --build --wait` returned only after `ollama-init` exited 0). `docs/STATUS.md` updated in this same commit; Free space left: 35 MB`, then a hard failure) -- not a code defect. Root cause: the runner's default free disk, already shared with several GB of preinstalled toolchains this repo never uses (Android SDK, .NET, GHC, CodeQL -- confirmed no workflow in this repo references any of them), didn't have room left for the two new services (`opensearch`, `ollama`) plus the two model tags `ollama-init` pulls into a volume at runtime, on top of the existing 12-service stack. Fixed by adding a step to the `e2e` job that reclaims those unused toolchains' disk (`rm -rf` the four directories, `docker image prune -af`) before `docker compose up`, rather than moving to a larger/paid runner tier for a portfolio project's CI. All other jobs on that same run (java, ai, python, web, dbt, audit-chain, reporting, terraform) were already green -- this was isolated to the one job whose Compose stack grew. Fixing the disk uncovered a second, distinct real bug in the same job, on the very next run: `docker compose up -d --build --wait` itself returned exit code 1 even though every container was actually healthy and `ollama-init` had exited 0 as designed -- confirmed from the raw log (`container infra-ollama-init-1 exited (0)` immediately followed by `Process completed with exit code 1`), not a flaky timing issue. Root cause, cross-checked against `docker/compose#10596` (a long-open upstream issue with the same exact symptom): `--wait` treats any container that exits during its convergence check as a failure unless another service's `depends_on` vouches for that exit via `condition: service_completed_successfully` -- `airflow-init` already has that (its two dependents declare it), which is why it never tripped this; `ollama-init` doesn't, because nothing in this compose file actually needs Ollama's models to start (only the `ai/` test suite, run outside Compose, does). Reproduced locally first (a plain re-run of `up --wait` against the already-running local stack failed identically -- this was a latent bug in Task 1's original compose design the first, disk-only local verification pass never actually exercised, not a GitHub-only difference), then fixed and re-verified locally, both before touching CI again: split into Compose's own documented pattern for exactly this case (`up --wait` over every service except `ollama-init`, then a separate `up -d ollama-init` + `compose wait ollama-init` to block on its own exit code) -- confirmed all three commands exit 0 locally against a real, already-populated stack. `infra/docker-compose.yml`'s `ollama-init` comment corrected to stop claiming "same shape as `airflow-init`," since that was true for the pull-idempotency point it was making but not for `--wait` semantics -- the exact kind of small inaccuracy this project's own convention says to fix rather than leave once found. Third push (`0035fa2`) confirmed fully green on GitHub Actions: all 9 jobs, including `e2e`, which ran its full 11m22s Compose-plus-both-test-suites cycle without the workaround needing a second try -- Phase 2 Task 1 is now actually done by this project's own standard, not just passing locally. |
| 2026-08-23 | Phase 2, Task 2 (Policy Q&A / explainability RAG) -- `make lint`, `make test` (full monorepo), plus `make e2e` verified as two independent full-stack runs rather than one combined run (see the real, hardware-forced reason in the last finding below) | All green -- Java 80 (was 75; +4 `CitizenDeterminationAccessTest`, +1 `IntakeControllerTest`), web 11 (was 7; +4 `PolicyQaPage`), data-platform 27 unit+integration (was 26; +1 bronze schema-evolution regression) + 9 `e2e` (including a real, live-triggered Airflow DAG run), `ai/` 9 unit + 15 `e2e` (was 5; +2 grounding-retry, +3 denial/general provenance, +5 retrieval regressions re-run clean), none regressed. Six real corrections found during implementation, each verified live rather than assumed: (1) **the plan's `unique` constraint on `person.keycloak_subject`** -- reading the existing `IntakeControllerTest`/`AbstractApiTest`/`AbstractPostgresTest` before writing the migration showed 7+ existing tests share one seeded citizen token against a JVM-wide Testcontainers Postgres with no reset between tests; a literal unique constraint would have broken all of them the first time a second application reused that same token. `V12__person_keycloak_identity.sql` uses a plain (non-unique) index instead, with a comment explaining why, and `IntakeService` stamps `keycloak_subject` only on the head-of-household person, matching how ownership is actually resolved (`household.head_person_id`). (2) **the real DMN trace shape** -- `decision_results` is a flat JSON map keyed by decision node name (`"Gross Income"`, `"Net Income Test"`, `"Determination"`, ...), with `Determination` itself nested as `{eligible, benefitAmount, reasonCode}` -- confirmed by querying a live Postgres row directly rather than assumed from the design doc's prose, before `service.py`'s `_DENIAL_CONTEXT` mapping and `CitizenController.trace()` were written against it. A stale hardcoded intake date copied from `AuthorizationTest`'s pre-auth-check-only test bodies also surfaced here: `IntakeService` stamps `effectiveFrom` from a real system clock (`ClockConfig`), so a hardcoded `2025-06-15` against "today" (2026-08-23) produced a genuine `422 SNAP-FY2025 does not cover household size 0` once a new test actually reached DMN evaluation; fixed to `LocalDate.now()`, cross-checked against `V4__seed_snap_parameters.sql` to confirm a real `SNAP-FY2026` parameter set actually covers today rather than papering over a deeper gap. (3) **`RELEVANCE_THRESHOLD = -10.0`** -- set from real cross-encoder scores measured against the live index (relevant top hits clustered roughly -4.8 to -9.6; a deliberately unrelated question scored -11.1 to -11.3), not a guessed probability-like cutoff, matching how Task 1 set its own thresholds empirically. (4) **a real RAG grounding failure, not a retrieval failure** -- `test_a_denied_determination_cites_the_real_income_section` failed with `citation_grounded([], [])`: `hybrid_search` had correctly returned 273.9(a) as the top hit (score 2.093, confirmed by querying it directly), but the model's free-text explanation cited no section at all and stated a fabricated "$8,300, 130% of the Federal poverty level for a household of two" figure nowhere in the trusted DMN data it was given -- design doc §2.2's "an ungrounded guess is worse than no answer" was only wired to the pre-generation retrieval-score check, not to the model actually using what it was given. `_retrieve_and_answer` now retries generation once when `_cited_sections()` comes back empty (retrieval already proved relevant text exists, so a first-attempt miss is most likely this small model's non-zero-temperature variance), and abstains — via the same `_abstain()` path and message the retrieval-side check already used -- if the retry is also uncited. Covered by two new deterministic tests (`TestGroundingRetryAndAbstention`) using a stub `LlmClient` that echoes a real cfr_section parsed out of its own prompt, so the test doesn't depend on when a flaky model happens to comply. (5) **the 120s Ollama generation timeout was measured too tight for this machine** -- Ollama's own request log showed every real `/api/generate` call in one run taking 1m10s-1m44s under this session's CPU contention (OpenSearch/Keycloak/portal-api sharing the host), with one call cut off at exactly `2m0s` and logged as a 500 once the client gave up; widened to 240s with real measured headroom, the same evidence-based-constant discipline as (3). (6) **a genuine cross-cutting bug this task's own schema change surfaced in Phase 1b's bronze layer, unrelated to Policy Q&A itself** -- the instant `person` gained `keycloak_subject`, Airflow's next real, live-triggered `canopica_pipeline` run failed its `extract` task with deltalake's `_internal.SchemaMismatchError: Cannot cast schema, number of fields does not match: 12 vs 11`: `extract_to_bronze`'s `select * from {table}` naturally picks up any new column, but plain `write_deltalake(..., mode="append")` is schema-strict by default and refused to append a 12-column frame onto bronze/person's existing 11-column history. This is a real, general bug -- any future column added to any bronze-covered table would hit it identically -- not specific to this migration, so it was fixed at the source: `write_deltalake(..., mode="append", schema_mode="merge")`, proven first by a new regression test (`test_extract_tolerates_a_source_table_gaining_a_column`, which reproduces the exact failure by writing a real extract's schema minus one column as a stand-in for "bronze data written before this migration existed," confirmed red before the fix and green after) and then by actually re-triggering the live Airflow DAG end to end, which required rebuilding the `airflow-scheduler`/`airflow-webserver` image (confirmed via `docker run --entrypoint python3` against the freshly built image) since that image bakes `canopica_data` in via `COPY`+`pip install` at build time, not a live mount -- a source-only fix silently would not have reached the running scheduler. CI's own clean-slate stack would never have caught this (no pre-existing bronze history for a fresh environment to conflict with) -- only this dev machine's real, accumulated state did, which is itself worth knowing about the limits of always-clean CI environments. Separately (not a code bug): this session's local Docker Desktop VM (~7.654GiB ceiling) was OOM-killing OpenSearch repeatedly once the full 13-service stack (this task added no new services, but Task 1's opensearch/ollama plus all of Phase 1b's stack plus this task's own heavier reindex-on-fixture-setup e2e tests, all resident at once) exceeded real available memory under peak load (confirmed each time via `docker compose ps` showing `Exited (137)` and `docker stats` showing total resident memory approaching the ceiling) -- rather than weaken the verification, `data-platform`'s e2e (9/9, including the live Airflow DAG re-run above) and `ai/`'s e2e (15/15) were each confirmed fully green as independent full-stack runs, since this machine cannot sustain both suites' simultaneous peak memory, and the full 13-service stack was restored afterward. `docs/STATUS.md` updated in this same commit; `README.md`'s architecture diagram checked -- no change, Policy Q&A is already named inside the existing generic "AI Layer" box and this task implements that capability behind it, the same call Task 1 made for retrieval. |

Downloaded the actual `compose-logs.txt` artifact from the failing run (`gh run download`) instead of reasoning from the pytest side alone: OpenSearch's own comment already on this env var (`infra/docker-compose.yml`) states the breaker "checks heap-usage percentage, not absolute size" -- a percentage-of-heap breaker means the fix is heap headroom, not just freeing other containers, which only helps indirectly (less contention for the same physical RAM the JVM draws from, not more heap for OpenSearch itself). (2) The CI stop-step widened to also free `jaeger`/`portal-web` (unneeded by anything `ai/`'s e2e suite checks, same reasoning as the five already stopped), for whatever additional margin they're worth on the actual runner. (3) `free -h` added before and after the stop-step and unconditionally (`if: always()`) after the `ai/` e2e step -- so if this recurs a third time, the fix is informed by this runner's actual available/used memory instead of another inference from the pytest side alone. Recorded as a dated correction in the design doc itself (same style as its own existing §2.1/§2.2 corrections), not silently implemented around: the real gap isn't "raw PII in silver," it's that a one-way hash forecloses the one thing a token vault is actually for -- recovering a real value under an explicit, audited need. | 2026-08-22 | Post-Task-4 CI investigation -- `./mvnw -pl portal -am test` (local, ×3 consecutive runs), `make lint`, plus two live CI failures and their reruns on GitHub Actions | A real, previously-latent bug, not a flake -- caught only because a routine follow-up push happened to land inside the ~4-6 hours every day where UTC has already rolled to the next calendar date but US Eastern (`canopica.timezone=America/New_York`, `application.yml`) hasn't. Three tests (`WorkerCaseControllerTest.workerNotHoldingTheActiveAssignmentGets403`, `VerificationControllerTest.workerNotHoldingTheActiveAssignmentGets403OnBothEndpoints`, `SupervisorControllerTest.reassignReplacesTheActiveAssignment`) seeded a pre-existing `case_assignment` row via raw SQL's `current_date`, evaluated by Postgres in the Testcontainers container's own (UTC) timezone -- while the application's own `CaseAssignmentService` always compares against `LocalDate.now(clock)` using Eastern time. During that daily window, a row written with `current_date` lands "tomorrow" from the application's point of view, so `findEffectiveOn` (`effectiveFrom <= asOf`) silently stops seeing it as active: `assignOnFirstTouch` then treats the household as unassigned and lets the *wrong* worker auto-claim it (403 became 200 -- a real access-control bypass in the test's own premise, not the shipped app, confirmed by grepping `portal/src/main` for `current_date` and finding zero occurrences: production code never touches Postgres's own clock, only `LocalDate.now(clock)`, so it was never exposed to this mismatch), and `reassign()`'s `activeAssignment(...).ifPresent(...)` found nothing to end, leaving two simultaneously-"active" rows. Reproduced deterministically on GitHub Actions (failed identically on both the original run and its rerun, 3/3) but not on this machine after 3 consecutive local runs during the same live window -- consistent with Docker Desktop for Mac's own container clock staying aligned to local wall-clock date while a bare Linux Docker daemon's containers default to UTC with no host-timezone sync. Fixed by removing `current_date` from all three call sites in favor of a new `CaseFixtures.insertCaseAssignment(jdbc, householdId, workerId)` helper using `BASE_EFFECTIVE_FROM` (a fixed past date, already this file's established pattern for exactly this "unambiguous regardless of clock" reason) -- sidesteps the whole bug class rather than picking a "less wrong" clock. A fourth, unrelated `current_date` usage (`EffectiveDatingConstraintTest`'s `program_request.requested_on`) was checked and left alone: it's never compared against any application-clock value, only satisfies a NOT NULL column so an unrelated benefit-month CHECK-constraint test can run, so it carries none of this risk. `./mvnw -pl portal -am test`: 65/65, ×3 consecutive local runs, all green (expected -- this bug needs the CI host's own UTC-vs-Eastern mismatch to manifest, not present locally). `make lint`: clean. Not folded into Task 4's own commit/row above since it's a pre-existing latent bug the CI push merely happened to surface, not something Task 4 introduced -- these three tests and their `current_date` pattern predate Task 4 entirely (written in Tasks 2/3). |
| 2026-08-22 | Phase 1b, Task 4 -- `make test`, `make lint`, then a real Docker Compose cycle: `make up` (fresh build, all 8 services), a manually-triggered `canopica_pipeline` DAG run via curl against Airflow's REST API watched to `success`, a real operational-vs-serving row-count cross-check, `uv run pytest -m e2e` (7 tests, including the new `test_airflow_dag.py` triggering its own independent run), a second `pytest -m e2e` run after a lint fix (its own further independent run) -- three real DAG runs total this task, all reaching `success`, `make down` | All green. `make test`: Java 65, web 7, Python 19 non-e2e, unchanged from Task 3, no regressions. `make lint`: `ruff`/`mypy --strict`/`tsc` all clean (after two real fixes -- see below). `pytest -m e2e`: 7 passed (4 pre-existing stack-smoke checks + the new Airflow-webserver health check + `test_end_to_end.py` + the new `test_airflow_dag.py`), both times run. New `infra/airflow/Dockerfile` (custom image, `FROM apache/airflow:2.10.5-python3.12`), `infra/airflow/dags/canopica_pipeline_dag.py` (TaskFlow `@task` for `extract`/`materialize`/`provision_metabase`, `BashOperator` for `dbt_build`, matching `canopica_data.pipeline`'s own four-stage order), three new Compose services, and a new `airflow` role/database in `infra/postgres/init/01-databases.sql` (Airflow's metadata DB gets its own least-privilege role rather than reusing `canopica_app`, since it's orchestration-internal and unrelated to the app's own operational data). `AIRFLOW__CORE__DAGS_ARE_PAUSED_AT_CREATION: "false"` so the DAG's own schedule actually fires without a manual unpause step -- without it, "runs on its own schedule" would have been silently false despite everything else working. Genuinely the most consequential finding this task: getting the custom image to build at all took five rounds of real `ResolutionImpossible` errors against Airflow's own published constraints file, each diagnosed from the actual pip error text, not guessed --  `canopica_data`'s own direct dependencies need newer versions than three incidental Airflow-constrained packages neither Airflow's core nor its default providers actually import at runtime in this image (deltalake 0.24.0 vs >=1.6.2 for Task 10's bronze layer -- a different major API generation, not a patch bump; duckdb 1.1.3 vs >=1.5.5; httpx 0.27.0 vs >=0.28.1), which cascaded into two of their own transitive pins (`pydantic_core`, `typing_extensions`) once those three were freed to resolve newer. One of these round-trips was a pure self-inflicted bug, not a real conflict: `pydantic-core` (hyphen) doesn't match the file's actual `pydantic_core` (underscore) line, so an earlier "fix" silently excluded nothing and the same error recurred until diffing the raw constraints file text directly caught it -- worth remembering for any future constraints-file exclusion in this repo, since PyPI normalizes hyphen/underscore for installs but a literal `grep` does not. A sixth conflict was structural, not incidental: `dbt-core>=1.12.3` requires `click>=8.3`, which genuinely conflicts with Airflow 2.10.5's own `click==8.1.8` (Airflow's CLI depends on it directly) -- no constraints-file exclusion fixes this, since both sides have a real claim on the version. Fixed by not sharing dbt with Airflow's environment at all: `dbt-core`/`dbt-duckdb` install into a fully isolated `/opt/dbt-venv` (its own `pip`, no constraint file needed since it shares nothing), and the `dbt_build` `BashOperator` calls that venv's `dbt` binary by absolute path -- correct, not a workaround, since dbt only ever runs as a subprocess CLI in this codebase (`canopica_data.pipeline` already shells out to it the same way) and is never imported as a Python module anywhere a shared click version would matter. A harmless, deliberately-accepted residual: Airflow's own bundled `pandas==2.1.4` wants `numpy<2`, but `canopica_data` needs `numpy>=2.5.2` (Task 9's synthetic generator) -- pip installs successfully with a post-hoc warning, not a blocking error, and nothing in this DAG uses any pandas-dependent Airflow feature, so left as-is rather than downgrading `canopica_data`'s own numpy floor to satisfy an unused bundled dependency. `make lint` caught two real issues in the new Python test, both fixed: three `E501` line-too-long violations (ruff, 100-char limit), and a `mypy --strict` catch that `psycopg`'s `fetchone()` can return `None` -- fixed with the same `assert ... is not None` guard `test_materialize.py` already established for this exact situation, not a new pattern. `test_airflow_dag.py`'s own cross-check assertion (`mart_total == operational_count`) is deliberately order-independent rather than asserting a specific count, since pytest's default alphabetical collection runs it *before* `test_end_to_end.py` -- confirmed empirically across the two live runs, first against a 0-determination stack (0 == 0) and then, after `test_end_to_end.py` had run, against a non-zero one, both passing correctly. Architecture diagram checked (checkpoint skill step 4): no change -- the README diagram's `data` subgraph was already labeled "ELT, orchestrated by Airflow" at design time, anticipating this exact task. `README.md`'s "Honest limitations" section was stale beyond just this task's own scope (still listed row-level authorization and external verification as unbuilt, both done since Tasks 2/3) -- removed all three now-resolved bullets and updated the "Status & roadmap" table's Phase 1b row from "Planned" to "In progress (4/10 tasks)", the same kind of opportunistic accuracy fix Task 9's own README refresh made. CI's own `e2e` job failed on first push, not from anything above -- `container infra-metabase-1 is unhealthy`, timing out its healthcheck at ~162s against a ~150s budget (`retries: 12`, `interval: 10s`, `start_period: 30s`), with metabase's own config completely untouched by this task. Diagnosis: the three new `airflow-*` services building and starting concurrently on the same runner leaves noticeably less CPU available for metabase's own JVM startup than before -- a resource-contention near-miss caused by the stack's added weight, not a metabase regression. `gh run rerun --failed` passed cleanly on the very next attempt (same commit, no code change), confirming the marginal-timing theory the same way Task 2's Maven-fetch flake was confirmed. Widened `retries: 12` to `20` (~230s budget) as a follow-up hardening commit rather than leaving the margin thin for every future task's own CI run against this now-heavier stack -- the fix is headroom, not a metabase config change, and costs nothing when contention isn't an issue. |

Activates `verification` (dormant since Phase 1a's Task 2, design doc §2.2): `IntakeService` now inserts one `INCOME`/`OUTSTANDING` row per submission, `due_on` set 10 days out per 7 CFR 273.2(f)(1)'s minimum-notice standard. Genuinely new: `V9__verification_response.sql` (`verification_response` -- outcome + raw mock payload, kept off `verification.status` itself so that column's existing CHECK constraint didn't need touching); `MockVerificationService` (`requestVerification` -- synchronously writes the response, moves `verification.status` to `RECEIVED`, appends the audit pair, all `@Transactional`; `resolveOutcome` -- deterministic, hashes `(personId, dataElement)` via `UUID.nameUUIDFromBytes` into one of `MATCHES`/`DISCREPANCY`/`UNAVAILABLE` so reruns against the same synthetic data reproduce, same property the DMN model already holds); `VerificationController` (`GET .../verifications`, `POST .../verifications/{id}/request`, both under `/api/program-requests/{id}/verifications`). Three real, non-obvious design choices beyond the plan's own text: (1) the plan says the caseload check is "reused" from Task 2 -- read literally, not just in spirit, since a second copy of an authorization policy is a real drift risk (a future change to one copy and not the other silently reopens a hole); `WorkerCaseController`'s private `checkCaseloadAccess` was promoted to a public method on `CaseAssignmentService` (now also taking a `WorkerRepository` dependency) and both controllers call the identical method -- `WorkerCaseController` lost its own `WorkerRepository` field/constructor param and four imports (`Worker`, `CaseAssignment`, `NoSuchElementException`, `AccessDeniedException`) that existed only for the method that moved. (2) `SecurityConfig`'s existing `GET /api/program-requests/*` rule matches exactly one path segment past the id (Spring's `PathPattern` semantics) -- it silently would not have covered the new nested `/verifications` routes, which would have fallen through to the chain's `.anyRequest().authenticated()` (any authenticated worker-realm token, no role check at all, looser than every sibling endpoint); added a dedicated `.requestMatchers("/api/program-requests/*/verifications/**").hasAnyRole("WORKER", "SUPERVISOR")`, and confirmed correct by the controller tests actually exercising real 403/200 behavior through Spring Security, not by re-reading the pattern. (3) `POST .../request` looks up `verificationId` via `findByProgramRequestId(id)` + a filter, not a bare `findById` -- without this, a worker holding the assignment on household A could supply a `verificationId` belonging to household B and act on it, since the caseload check only validates against A's household; a fifth new test (`verificationIdNotBelongingToTheProgramRequestReturns404`) proves the mismatch is rejected rather than silently crossing households. One test-writing pitfall caught before commit, not a production bug: `MockVerificationServiceTest`'s first draft asserted a jsonb audit payload against Jackson's compact `"key":"value"` form, but Postgres's own jsonb-to-text canonicalization always inserts a space after `:`/`,` (documented in `V6__audit_event.sql`'s own trigger comment, and the reason `AuditChainTest` already asserts against bare keys rather than full pairs) -- fixed the assertion to match Postgres's canonical output, not the app's own pre-storage serialization. Architecture diagram checked (checkpoint skill step 4): no change -- verification lives inside the README diagram's existing "Customer / Worker Portal" / "System of record" boxes, same reasoning Tasks 1/2 already used; this task adds an endpoint and two tables inside the existing Portal tier, not a new top-level component or data flow. |

Genuinely new: `V8__household_sensitivity.sql` (`household.is_sensitive`/`sensitive_reason`, both mapped `insertable=false, updatable=false` on the entity -- settable only through the new SUPERVISOR-only endpoint, never through `Household`'s own constructor/save path); `CaseAssignmentService` (`assignOnFirstTouch` -- no-op if a household already has an active assignment, otherwise claims it for the viewing worker; `reassign` -- SUPERVISOR-only, ends the old assignment and starts a new one; `flagSensitivity`); a new `SupervisorController` (`POST .../reassign`, `PUT .../sensitivity`, both gated `hasRole("SUPERVISOR")` in `SecurityConfig`). `WorkerCaseController.caseDetail` now runs a caseload check before returning: a WORKER auto-claims an unassigned household (real `case_assignment` row, not just a read) or is denied outright (`AccessDeniedException`, translated to 403 by Spring Security's existing `oauth2ResourceServer` handler -- not a new exception mapping) if someone else already holds it; a SUPERVISOR always gets in, with the override recorded via `CASE_VIEWED`'s payload gaining `in_assignment: boolean` (previously always an empty map). `SecurityConfig`'s worker-realm chain widened `GET /api/program-requests/*`, `GET /api/determinations/*/trace`, and `/api/worker/**` from `hasRole("WORKER")` to `hasAnyRole("WORKER", "SUPERVISOR")` -- the row-level check itself stays data-driven in the controller, this only lets the SUPERVISOR role reach those endpoints at all; determination *creation* stays WORKER-only on purpose, Task 2 doesn't widen it. One real, non-obvious design choice beyond the plan's own text: `CaseAssignmentRepository.findEffectiveOn` gained `order by effective_from desc, created_at desc` -- a same-day reassignment can legitimately leave the old row's `effective_to` and the new row's `effective_from` both equal to "today," which without an explicit order left which row `.findFirst()` saw undefined (Postgres gives no ordering guarantee otherwise); this makes "most recently created wins" deterministic instead of leaving it to chance. One real bug caught by the suite, not just the new tests: `SupervisorController.setSensitivity` calling `HouseholdRepository.updateSensitivity` (a `@Modifying` query) directly threw `TransactionRequiredException` -- Spring Data JPA refuses to run a modifying query outside a transaction, and a `@RestController` method isn't one by default. Fixed by moving the write into `CaseAssignmentService.flagSensitivity` (`@Transactional`), the same pattern `reassign` already used, rather than annotating the controller directly -- keeps the established convention in this codebase that services own transactions, controllers stay thin. `AuthorizationTest.supervisorTokenIsForbiddenFromTheWorkerCaseList` (Task 1's own comment already flagged this as expected to change) is now `supervisorTokenIsAuthorizedForTheWorkerCaseList` (200); the genuine role-mismatch 403 case moved to a SUPERVISOR token against determination creation, which Task 2 deliberately leaves WORKER-only. No Docker Compose full-stack cycle run this task, unlike Task 1 -- Task 2 adds no new service, env var, or cross-container address to `infra/docker-compose.yml`, so there's no analogue to Task 1's issuer-vs-jwks-uri class of bug for a Compose run to catch that Testcontainers' identical Flyway migrations and identical Spring context wouldn't already cover; same reasoning Phase 1a's Tasks 2/3/5/6/7 already relied on before Compose existed at all. | 2026-08-22 | Phase 1b, Task 1 -- `./mvnw -pl portal -am test` (Testcontainers Keycloak), `make test`, `make lint`, then a real Docker Compose cycle: `make up`, `uv run pytest -m e2e`, `make seed` (500 households through the real citizen-authenticated intake path), a live Playwright browser session (citizen login -> real submission, worker login -> real case list -> real determination run -> sign-out), `make down` | All green. `make test`: Java 55 (43 portal, up from 41 -- net new `AuthorizationTest` cases; 12 rules-engine, unchanged), web 7, Python 19 non-e2e, no regressions. `make lint`: `ruff`/`mypy`/`tsc` all clean. `pytest -m e2e`: 5 passed (4 stack-smoke incl. the new Keycloak realm-descriptor check, plus the full intake-through-mart test, now authenticating with a real worker token). Real OIDC replaces `HardcodedRoleFilter`: two Keycloak realms (`identity/realm-export/canopica-workers-realm.json`, `canopica-citizens-realm.json`, hand-written and reviewable, not a raw `kc.sh export`), two Spring Security resource-server chains in `SecurityConfig` matched by request path (POST `/api/applications` -> citizens realm, everything else -> workers realm), `KeycloakWorkerSyncFilter` auto-provisions a `worker` row from the JWT's own claims on first login (new `V7__worker_keycloak_identity.sql` adds `worker.keycloak_subject`; `worker`/`case_assignment`/`worker.role`'s `WORKER`/`SUPERVISOR`/`ADMIN` values already existed, unused, since Phase 1a's Task 2). React gets a real login flow (`src/auth/`, `react-oidc-context`, Authorization Code + PKCE) replacing the old client-side role-header switch -- a realm-choice landing screen, then a real Keycloak redirect per realm. Three real, non-obvious bugs found by actually running this against real infrastructure, not just Testcontainers: (1) `JwtDecoders.fromIssuerLocation()` performs its OIDC-discovery HTTP call eagerly, at bean-creation time -- broke every *other* test class booting the same `@SpringBootTest` context without needing MockMvc/security at all (schema tests, policy-parameter tests), since they have no Keycloak container and Spring context startup failed on an unrelated dependency; fixed with a decoder that defers construction until first `decode()` call, so a test that never makes an authenticated request never pays for it. (2) Genuinely the more consequential one: **against the real Compose stack**, every request failed 401 ("Another algorithm expected, or no matching key(s) found") -- Keycloak stamps a token's `iss` claim as whichever address the *token-requesting* caller used to reach it, and every real caller (browser, the e2e test, `make seed`'s loader) runs on the host at `localhost:8081`, while portal-api's own `CANOPICA_KEYCLOAK_*_ISSUER_URI` env vars pointed at the container-network `keycloak:8080` -- a real mismatch, not a flake, since `fromIssuerLocation()` uses one address for both discovery *and* issuer validation. Fixed by splitting `canopica.keycloak.{realm}-issuer-uri` (a pure comparison string, stays `localhost:8081` in both local dev and Compose) from a new `canopica.keycloak.{realm}-jwks-uri` (a real network address, overridden to `keycloak:8080` only in `infra/docker-compose.yml`) via `NimbusJwtDecoder.withJwkSetUri(...)` + `JwtValidators.createDefaultWithIssuer(...)` instead of the one-address convenience method -- caught only because Task 1's own plan called for verifying against the real Compose service, not just Testcontainers. (3) Found live-browser-testing sign-out (an interaction no automated test exercised): clicking "Sign out" silently re-triggered a second Keycloak round-trip before returning to the realm-choice screen, since `removeUser()` resolves asynchronously and the app's own "not authenticated, redirect" effect fired again in that window -- invisible to the user only because Keycloak's SSO session was still alive, so it round-tripped silently rather than showing a login form, leaving a stale `?code=...&state=...` in the URL. Fixed with a mount-scoped `useRef` guard so auto-redirect fires once per login attempt, not on every render where `isAuthenticated` happens to be `false`; re-verified via the same live browser flow, clean the second time. `make seed`'s `canopica_data.synthetic.loader.post_households` and `test_end_to_end.py` both updated to fetch a real token from the `test-customer`/`test-worker` confidential clients (Direct Access Grant) instead of sending `X-Canopica-Role`, the same "hit the real thing" standard as everything else these tests already hold to. Architecture diagram checked (checkpoint skill step 4): no change needed -- the README's diagram already shows "Portal" as one box; Keycloak lives inside that tier, and this task didn't add a new top-level component or data flow at that diagram's level of abstraction (the roadmap doc's own, more detailed §3.1 diagram already depicted Keycloak from the design phase). `README.md`'s "Honest limitations" bullet on identity corrected (no longer describes removed behavior); `CLAUDE.md`'s "Current status" line updated -- both minimal, task-scoped edits, not the phase-wrap-up rewrite Task 13's own pattern used, since only Task 1 of 10 is done. |

Fixed by giving each run its own random `benefit_month` (decoupled from `as_of`, which the real system doesn't tie it to either), then proved the fix by running the e2e test three times in a row against one live stack with zero teardown between runs, all green. Java: +8 tests over Task 2 — as-of parameter resolution across the FY2025/FY2026 boundary (exact, not off-by-one), size-scoped vs. scalar parameter distinction, missing-size and missing-date rejection, and database-enforced immutability (UPDATE/DELETE on published parameter sets refused). FY2025 and FY2026 SNAP figures (max allotments, standard deductions, gross/net income limits, excess shelter cap, minimum benefit) verified directly against USDA FNS's published COLA memos before seeding — see docs/design/policy-parameter-provenance.md for citations; statutory figures (20% earned-income deduction, $35 medical threshold, 50% shelter share, 30% benefit reduction, 2-person minimum-benefit cutoff) cited to 7 U.S.C./7 CFR since they aren't in the COLA memos. One regression caught and fixed: Task 1's `CanopicaPortalApplicationTest` excluded JPA/DataSource autoconfiguration (valid when written, before any database existed) and broke once Task 3 added a `@Component` requiring JPA repositories — converted to extend `AbstractPostgresTest` like every other test since Task 2, consistent with that class's own doc comment. |
| 2026-08-21 | Task 6 -- `make test` (Java 31 tests, Python 4 tests incl. 2 real Postgres-integration), `make lint` | All green. Java: +4 tests -- the chain's first row links from the zero-hash, every row links to its predecessor, UPDATE/DELETE both refused with "append-only", and every real determination appends exactly one DETERMINATION_MADE event carrying the policy parameter version in its payload. Python: a new `verify_chain()` CLI/library (`canopica_data.audit.verify_chain`) plus two integration tests against a Postgres instance running the portal's *actual* Flyway migrations (via the `flyway/flyway` Docker image, not a copied-SQL approximation) -- proves an untampered 5-row chain verifies, and that a superuser directly disabling the trigger and editing a row is still caught (the verifier detects what no application-level control could have prevented). New CI job `audit-chain` runs this against GitHub Actions' own Docker daemon. One environment-specific issue found and fixed, not a code bug: Testcontainers-Python's Ryuk reaper container fails to start on this machine's Docker Desktop for macOS ("error while creating mount source path ...docker.sock: operation not supported") -- a known Docker-Desktop-macOS quirk affecting testcontainers-python specifically (the Java-side Testcontainers tests are unaffected). Worked around via `TESTCONTAINERS_RYUK_DISABLED=true`, scoped to the Makefile's Python test invocation only (not CI, which doesn't hit this on Linux runners) -- documented in `tests/conftest.py`'s module docstring so a future session recognizes it immediately rather than re-diagnosing. Also switched off the deprecated `testcontainers.postgres` import path to `testcontainers.community.postgres` while touching this file. |
| 2026-08-21 | Task 5 — `make test`, `make lint` (portal now runs 27 Testcontainers tests) | All green. Java: +10 tests over Task 4 -- fact assembly (as-of exclusion of not-yet-effective income, inclusion of later-effective income, elderly-by-age-60 and disability-record detection, SSI-driven categorical eligibility), determination persistence (determination + trace both written, append-only enforced, a denial stores a zero benefit and the correct reason code), and the exact property CLAUDE.md's testing policy names for the rules engine: an old determination re-run against its own stored parameter-set version reproduces its original answer even after both the household's income and the fiscal year have since moved on, plus a determination made on/after October 1 picks up the new fiscal year. All passed on the first real run -- no logic bugs, only two known Jackson/Hibernate wiring points handled up front: `@JdbcTypeCode(SqlTypes.JSON)` on the two jsonb-backed trace columns (plain String mapping fails Hibernate's ddl-auto=validate against a jsonb column), and SnapPolicyParameters resolution split into a shared `buildFromSet` helper so reproduction can pin to a stored parameter_set_id instead of re-resolving by "today's" date. |
| 2026-08-21 | Task 4 — `./mvnw -pl rules-engine test` (rules-engine module) | All green: 12 tests, 0 failures. 2 DMN model sanity tests (compiles clean, every named decision appears in the trace) + 9 table-driven SNAP scenarios (gross/net income pass/fail, each deduction in order, categorical eligibility, elderly/disabled uncapped shelter deduction, minimum-benefit floor, "computes to zero is a denial not a $0 award") + 1 as-of-date-correctness test (same facts, two parameter versions, different benefits -- proves parameters are injected, not baked in). All 12 passed on the first real run after two build-time fixes (Class-vs-ClassLoader argument to kie-dmn's classpath loader; a missing JUnit import) and one XML-syntax fix (an illegal double-hyphen inside an XML comment, unrelated to DMN semantics) -- no arithmetic mismatches, meaning the hand-computed expected values and the fifteen-decision DMN model agreed on every scenario. `make test`/`make lint` (full project) also green. |
